| Caused by | - Expansion of [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative "Royal prerogative") and _[lèse majesté](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A8se_majest%C3%A9_in_Thailand "Lèse majesté in Thailand")_<br>- Democratic and economic regression since [2014 Thai coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "2014 Thai coup d'état")<br>- Dissolution of the pro-democracy [Future Forward Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Forward_Party "Future Forward Party")<br>- Distrust in the [2019 general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Thai_general_election "2019 Thai general election") and the current political system, [competitive authoritarianism and illiberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiberal_democracy "Illiberal democracy")<br>- Forced disappearance and deaths of political activists, including [Wanchalearm Satsaksit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanchalearm_Satsaksit "Wanchalearm Satsaksit")<br>- [Political corruption scandals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Thailand "Corruption in Thailand"), including [Network monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Network_monarchy "Network monarchy") and [1MDB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1MDB "1MDB")[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc32-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-bbc32)[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-5)<br>- [legal inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equality_before_the_law "Equality before the law") and [human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Thailand "Human rights in Thailand")<br>- Economic impact of [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Thailand "COVID-19 pandemic in Thailand") and government response<br>- [LGBTQ discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Thailand "LGBTQ rights in Thailand")<br>- Dissatisfaction with [education system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Thailand "Education in Thailand") |  |
| Goals | - Dissolution of the [House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_(Thailand) "House of Representatives (Thailand)") and fresh legislative elections<br>- Drafting a new constitution<br>  - Abolition of the military-appointed [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Thailand "Senate of Thailand")<br>- Restriction of royal prerogative and abolition of _lèse majesté_ laws<br>- Increasing civil, economic and political rights |  |
| Methods | [Demonstrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonstration_(political) "Demonstration (political)"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-7) [sit-ins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sit-in "Sit-in"), [flash mob](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flash_mob "Flash mob"), [online activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_activism "Online activism"), [petition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petition "Petition"), [protest art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protest_art "Protest art"), [consumer activism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumer_activism "Consumer activism"), [hunger strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunger_strike "Hunger strike"), [self-harm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-harm "Self-harm"), [initiative campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_initiative "Popular initiative") |  |
| Result | Protesters dispersed<br>- "Severe" [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") declared in Bangkok from 15–22 October 2020<br>- Protesters' demands, including calls for constitutional amendment, failed.<br>- Constitutional Court rules that proposing reform of the monarchy is unconstitutional and amounts to acting to overthrow it[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-8)<br>- Suppression and surveillance of key protesters. |  |

In [Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thailand "Thailand"), protests began in early 2020 with demonstrations against the government of Prime Minister [Prayut Chan-o-cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayut_Chan-o-cha "Prayut Chan-o-cha"). They later expanded to include the unprecedented demands for reform of the [Thai monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Thailand "Monarchy of Thailand"). The protests were initially triggered by the dissolution of the [Future Forward Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Forward_Party "Future Forward Party") (FFP) in late February 2020 which was critical of Prayut, the changes to the [Thai constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Thailand "Constitution of Thailand") in 2017, and the country's political landscape that it gave rise to.

This first wave of protests was held exclusively on academic campuses and was brought to a halt by the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Thailand "COVID-19 pandemic in Thailand"). Protests resumed on 18 July 2020 with a large demonstration organised under the Free Youth umbrella at the [Democracy Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Monument "Democracy Monument") in [Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok "Bangkok"). Three demands were presented to the [Government of Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_Thailand "Government of Thailand"): the [dissolution of parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_parliament "Dissolution of parliament"), ending intimidation of the people, and the drafting of a new constitution. The July protests were triggered by the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic and enforcement of the lockdown Emergency Decree and spread nationwide. The protesters were mostly students and young people without an overall leader.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-44)

On 3 August, two student groups publicly raised demands to reform the monarchy, breaking a long taboo of publicly criticising the monarchy. A week later, ten demands for monarchy reform were declared. A 19 September rally saw 5,000–10,000 protesters[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ratcliffe_2020-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-Ratcliffe_2020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-46) and has been described as an open challenge to [King Vajiralongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiralongkorn "Vajiralongkorn"). A government decision to delay voting on a constitutional amendment in late September fuelled nearly unprecedented public republican sentiment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[h\]](#refnote-47) Following mass protests on 14 October, a "severe" [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") was declared in Bangkok during 15–22 October citing the alleged blocking of a royal motorcade. During this period saw the first crackdown by police on 16 October using [water cannons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_cannon "Water cannon").

In November, the Parliament voted to pass two constitutional amendment bills, but their content effectively shut down the protesters' demands of abolishing the Senate and reformation of the monarchy. Clashes between the protesters and the police and royalists became more prevalent, and resulted in many injuries. Protests in 2021 were more sporadic compared to the previous year, with prominent protesters facing charges and the police using harsher tactics. The first protester died in October 2021, with the video showing that he was shot with live rounds by a person inside a police station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-warit-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-warit)

Government responses included filing criminal charges using the Emergency Decree; arbitrary detention and police intimidation; delaying tactics; the deployment of [military information warfare units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Security_Operations_Command "Internal Security Operations Command"); media censorship; the mobilisation of pro-government and royalist groups who have accused the protesters of receiving support from foreign governments or [non-governmental organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-governmental_organization "Non-governmental organization") (NGOs) as part of a conspiracy against Thailand; and the deployment of thousands of police at protests. The government ordered university chancellors to prevent students from demanding reforms to the monarchy and to identify student protest leaders. Protests since October, when the King had returned to the country from Germany,[&#8202;](#ref-back-bengali_16102020-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-bengali_16102020) resulted in the deployment of the military, riot police, and mass arrests. In November 2021, the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand "Constitutional Court of Thailand") ruled that demands for reform of the monarchy were unconstitutional and ordered an end to all movements. The ruling has been likened to [judicial coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Coup "Constitutional Coup").[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardiannovember-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-guardiannovember)

By the end of 2021, the leading figures [Arnon Nampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Nampa "Arnon Nampa"), [Panupong Jadnok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panupong_Jadnok "Panupong Jadnok"), [Parit Chiwarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parit_Chiwarak "Parit Chiwarak"), [Jatupat Boonpattararaksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jatupat_Boonpattararaksa "Jatupat Boonpattararaksa"), [Panusaya Sithijirawattanakul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panusaya_Sithijirawattanakul "Panusaya Sithijirawattanakul"), and [Benja Apan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benja_Apan "Benja Apan") were all detained awaiting trial in series of [detainments and releases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_arrest_and_detention "Arbitrary arrest and detention"), some were imprisoned accumulatively for more than 200 days since 2020, after Prayut declared the use of all laws, including [lèse-majesté](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A8se-majest%C3%A9_in_Thailand "Lèse-majesté in Thailand"), against the protesters. By 2022, street protests largely died down due to government suppression and internal divisions, without any demands responded, although small-scale and online activism continued.

The protests were identified, as early as in 2020, as " [Gen Z protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gen_Z_protests "Gen Z protests")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-51)

### Direct causes

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Direct causes") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section's source code: Direct causes")\]

Over the previous 90 years in Thailand, elected governments have frequently been overthrown by military [coups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup "Coup").[&#8202;](#ref-back-paddock_21102020-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-paddock_21102020) Thirteen successful coups have occurred since the end of [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy "Absolute monarchy") in the [revolution of 1932](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siamese_revolution_of_1932 "Siamese revolution of 1932").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ratclifffe_13102020-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-ratclifffe_13102020) As head of the [Royal Thai Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Army "Royal Thai Army"), Prayut Chan-o-cha instigated the most recent [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "2014 Thai coup d'état") in 2014, and was leader of the [National Council for Peace and Order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_for_Peace_and_Order "National Council for Peace and Order") (NCPO), the [military junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_junta "Military junta") which came to power following the coup. Prayut was eventually appointed [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Thailand "Prime Minister of Thailand") and, together with the NCPO, ruled the country for five years, during which political and civil rights were restricted and economic inequality widened.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-54)[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-55) A [disputed referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Thai_constitutional_referendum "2016 Thai constitutional referendum"), widely called unfree and unfair,[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-56) was held in 2016 to approve a [new military-drafted constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_constitution_of_Thailand "2017 constitution of Thailand"). Analysts have described the new constitution as favouring the military and disadvantaging large political parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-57-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-57) It includes a junta-appointed Senate, empowered to vote for the prime minister for five years, allowing the military to select two prime ministers in the future,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02) and binds future governments to a 20-year national strategy 'road map' laid down the NCPO, effectively locking the country into the period of [military-guided democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guided_democracy "Guided democracy") with a much reduced role for politicians at both national and local levels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-59)

Prayut's supporters make up a majority of parliament.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-60) The [2019 Thai general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Thai_general_election "2019 Thai general election"), which was considered "partly free and not fair" and an example of [electoral authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_authoritarianism "Electoral authoritarianism"), was described as a 'political ritual'[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-1)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02) that nominally brought an end to the NCPO. The political system was continued with the formation of a new civil-military party, [Palang Pracharat Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palang_Pracharath_Party "Palang Pracharath Party"), which continued the NCPO's policies and orders in a form of [competitive authoritarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Competitive_authoritarianism "Competitive authoritarianism").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-2)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02) The coalition government is composed of pro-Prayut camps and smaller parties who benefited from multiple technical interpretations of the election law by a military-controlled Election Commission, including a 44-day hiatus while the election laws were reinterpreted to pave way for a coalition with the state military party at the helm.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-61)[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-63) Via NCPO mechanisms, Prayut has appointed allies to the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Thailand "Senate of Thailand"), [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand "Constitutional Court of Thailand"), various [Constitutional organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_organizations_of_Thailand "Constitutional organizations of Thailand"), including the [Election Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_Commission_of_Thailand "Election Commission of Thailand"), and the [National Anti-Corruption Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anti-Corruption_Commission_(Thailand) "National Anti-Corruption Commission (Thailand)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-3)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02) as well as officials at the [local government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_of_the_government_of_Thailand "Organization of the government of Thailand") level. Substantively amending the Constitution is almost impossible as it would require both Senate support and a referendum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-64) Numerous generals, as well as people with historical links to [organized crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_crime "Organized crime") (e.g., [Thammanat Prompao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamanat_Prompow "Thamanat Prompow")[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-65)), hold key ministerial positions in Second Prayut Cabinet.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-4)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-:1)

During the 2019 general election, the [FFP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Forward_Party "Future Forward Party") was received well by progressives and youths, who viewed it as an alternative to traditional political parties and as against the NCPO,[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-68) revealing a socio-political [cleavage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleavage_(politics) "Cleavage (politics)") along generational lines,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-5)[\[50\]](#refnote-:02) i.e., between Thai youth and the ruling Thai [gerontocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerontocracy "Gerontocracy"). The party won the third-largest share of parliamentary seats.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-:12) After eleven months of the coalition, an opposition FFP became short-lived when it was dissolved by the Constitutional Court, as the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Representatives_(Thailand) "House of Representatives (Thailand)") about to debate on [motion of no confidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_no_confidence "Motion of no confidence").[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-70) Former FFP members stressed the regime's corruption and were active in exposing the junta's involvement in the [1MDB scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1MDB_scandal "1MDB scandal").[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc32-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-bbc32)

### Underlying causes

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Underlying causes") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section's source code: Underlying causes")\]

Further sources of grievance, many of which the FFP championed, include [abortion rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion-rights_movements "Abortion-rights movements"); authoritarianism in Thai schools (including [hazing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hazing "Hazing")); education reform; [labour rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_rights "Labor rights") (trade unionism); military reform (e.g., ending [conscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription "Conscription") and reducing the defense budget, including the purchase of submarines[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-71)), [monopolies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monopoly "Monopoly") (e.g., alcohol), and women's rights.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-:2)

Criticism of the monarchy was quite rare during the 70-year reign of King [Bhumibol Adulyadej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhumibol_Adulyadej "Bhumibol Adulyadej"), which ended in 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters_10082020-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-reuters_10082020) After the period of decreased status of the monarchy during [Khana Ratsadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khana_Ratsadon "Khana Ratsadon") era, he restored influence and respect to the throne, which helped define the modern Thai political landscape.[&#8202;](#ref-back-solomon_26102020-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-solomon_26102020) Since assuming the throne, King [Vajiralongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiralongkorn "Vajiralongkorn") has intervened publicly in Thai political affairs. The King voiced his opinion on the Constitution in 2017, leading to an amendment on the power of the monarchy in the version that had already been accepted in the [2016 constitutional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Thai_constitutional_referendum "2016 Thai constitutional referendum").[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters_13012017-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-reuters_13012017) He became one of the world's richest monarchs in 2018, when he was granted personal ownership of royal assets from the [Crown Property Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crown_Property_Bureau "Crown Property Bureau") (CPB), valued at approximately US$40billion, which was formerly legally considered publicly owned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kings_money_ft-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-kings_money_ft) He has also consolidated the [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_Thailand "Privy Council of Thailand"), Office of the Royal Household and Royal Security Office into a single personal office;[&#8202;](#ref-back-:9-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-:9) in 2020, the government transferred two army units to his personal command,[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-78) giving military control to the monarchy which was unprecedented in modern Thailand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-79) The Royal Office's budget for 2020 was US$290million, more than double its budget from 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kings_money_ft-1)[\[68\]](#refnote-kings_money_ft)

On the eve of the 2019 election, Vajiralongkorn issued a royal announcement urging people to vote for "good people" ( [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language"): คนดี; [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription "Royal Thai General System of Transcription"):_khon di_; i.e., the junta parties), which was re-broadcast the following morning, in an "unprecedented intervention by the palace".[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[59\]](#refnote-:1):97 This sparked a massive, immediate, negative reaction on Twitter by Thai youth, using the hashtag "We are grown-ups and can choose for ourselves" ( [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language"): โตแล้วเลือกเองได้; [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription "Royal Thai General System of Transcription"):_to laeo lueak eng dai_).[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-80)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-2)[\[59\]](#refnote-:1) Following the election, on 19 July 2019, when the new cabinet was sworn in, they pledged their allegiance to the monarchy, but left out an oath to the constitution, and despite protests, did not correct what was widely seen as a serious breach of the traditional [oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office "Oath of office") and a tacit admission of the increasingly absolutist nature of the Thai monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0) Subsequently, in a ceremony on 27 August, each minister was presented with a framed message of support from the King.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-2)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0) He has also been accused of rewriting history as monuments associated with Khana Ratsadon and the revolution of 1932 were demolished.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-3)[\[58\]](#refnote-:0)

Vajiralongkorn resides most of the time in the state of [Bavaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bavaria "Bavaria"), Germany.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kings_money_ft-2)[\[68\]](#refnote-kings_money_ft)[&#8202;](#ref-back-saudelli_16102020-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-saudelli_16102020) At his request, the Thai constitution was amended to remove the requirement that a [regent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regent "Regent") needed to be appointed. According to the German foreign ministry, the Thai ambassador has been told multiple times that Germany opposes "having guests in our country who run their state affairs from here."[&#8202;](#ref-back-saudelli_16102020-1)[\[73\]](#refnote-saudelli_16102020)

The implementation of the [Thai lèse majesté law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A8se_majest%C3%A9_in_Thailand "Lèse majesté in Thailand") has been controversial since Bhumibol's reign. The number of cases peaked to an unprecedented scale after 2014 coup.[&#8202;](#ref-back-2014highest-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-2014highest) Critics viewed it as a political weapon to suppress dissent and restrict [freedom of speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_speech_in_Thailand "Freedom of speech in Thailand"). Even though there have been no new cases since 2018, as Prayut said was the wish of King Vajiralongkorn, other security laws had been invoked in its place, such as the [sedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedition "Sedition") law, the Computer Crime Act, or the offense of being a member of an [organized crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organized_crime "Organized crime") group (อั้งยี่), all of which incur comparably severe punishment, and has been using this law as a political manipulation tool.[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-83) In June 2020, the forced disappearance of [Wanchalearm Satsaksit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wanchalearm_Satsaksit "Wanchalearm Satsaksit"), assumed to be related to accusations of lèse majesté, gained online attention and sympathy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-84) In July 2020, [Tiwagorn Withiton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiwagorn_Withiton "Tiwagorn Withiton"), who wore a shirt with the slogan "I lost faith in the monarchy", was subjected to [political psychiatry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_abuse_of_psychiatry "Political abuse of psychiatry").[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-85)

### Concurrent developments

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Concurrent developments") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section's source code: Concurrent developments")\]

Further information: [COVID-19 pandemic in Thailand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Thailand "COVID-19 pandemic in Thailand")

The government invoked the Emergency Decree on 26 March and issued a COVID-19 related curfew on 3 April in order to limit the spread of the virus.[&#8202;](#ref-back-86-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-86)[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-87) The government additionally issued a travel ban for all foreigners entering Thailand. International rights groups have criticized the emergency decree being employed to suppress free speech.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-88) Even though the country has a relatively successful response, as of July 2020, contributed by its robust [public health infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Thailand "Healthcare in Thailand"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-89)[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-90) the state of emergency and the severe economic restriction has not been cancelled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-91)[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[i\]](#refnote-93) The country's significant tourism industry has been heavily affected, leaving Thailand with its worst economic downturn since the [1997 Asian financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_Asian_financial_crisis "1997 Asian financial crisis").[&#8202;](#ref-back-solomon_26102020-1)[\[66\]](#refnote-solomon_26102020) The [International Monetary Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Monetary_Fund "International Monetary Fund") has predicted Thailand's GDP to shrink by 6.7 percent in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-94) The government borrowed and announced a 1.9trillion- [baht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baht "Baht") (US$60billion) stimulus package, though few people have actually received it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-95)

Shortly before the second wave protests, on 15 July, netizens were infuriated by privileged treatment to "VIP guests" who were later revealed to be positive for the coronavirus,[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-96) as well as its failure to boost the heavily affected tourism industry.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-97)[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-98) On the same day, [Prayut Chan-o-cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayut_Chan-o-cha "Prayut Chan-o-cha") made a visit to [Rayong Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rayong_Province "Rayong Province"). Two protesters held signs calling for his resignation prior to the arrival; both were immediately arrested and reportedly beaten by the police, infuriating many [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") users.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-99)

Other related developments include the [suicide of senior judge Khanakorn Pianchana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suicide_of_Khanakorn_Pianchana "Suicide of Khanakorn Pianchana") over his frustration due to pressure on his verdicts in favour of military officers, [surgical mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgical_mask "Surgical mask") profiteering by [Thammanat Prompao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamanat_Prompow "Thamanat Prompow"), delayed COVID-19 welfare money transfers, the government's approval of the Civil Partnership bill (which does not recognize equal status of same-sex couples), and the case against [Red Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Bull "Red Bull") heir [Vorayuth Yoovidhya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vorayuth_Yoovidhya "Vorayuth Yoovidhya").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-:2)

## First wave (February 2020)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=5 "Edit section: First wave (February 2020)") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section's source code: First wave (February 2020)")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1e/%E0%B8%A1%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A7_%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B8%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%99%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%99_%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%97%E0%B9%89%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%B8%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%95%E0%B9%83%E0%B8%AB%E0%B8%A1%E0%B9%88_01.jpg/250px-%E0%B8%A1%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A7_%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B8%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%99%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%99_%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%97%E0%B9%89%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%B8%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%95%E0%B9%83%E0%B8%AB%E0%B8%A1%E0%B9%88_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%E0%B8%A1%E0%B8%A8%E0%B8%A7_%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B8%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%99%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%99_%E0%B8%9B%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%97%E0%B9%89%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%B8%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%99%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%95%E0%B9%83%E0%B8%AB%E0%B8%A1%E0%B9%88_01.jpg) Protests at [Srinakharinwirot University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinakharinwirot_University "Srinakharinwirot University") [Ongkharak Campus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ongkharak_District "Ongkharak District") on 25 February. The crossed out number is the popular vote count of dissolved [FFP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_Forward_Party "Future Forward Party")

The first wave protests were triggered by the Constitution Court decision to disband the FFP on 23 February 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-100) Demonstrations since erupted in various high schools, colleges, and universities nationwide. These student-organised protests also came with various [hashtags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashtag "Hashtag") unique to their institutions. The firsts began at [Thammasat University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thammasat_University "Thammasat University"), [Chulalongkorn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn_University "Chulalongkorn University"), [Ramkhamhaeng University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramkhamhaeng_University "Ramkhamhaeng University"), [Kasetsart University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasetsart_University "Kasetsart University"), [Srinakharinwirot University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinakharinwirot_University "Srinakharinwirot University") and [Prince of Songkhla University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_of_Songkhla_University "Prince of Songkhla University") on 24 February. Various high school students also organised protests at [Triam Udom Suksa School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triam_Udom_Suksa_School "Triam Udom Suksa School") and [Suksanari School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suksanari_School "Suksanari School").

The protests, however, were limited to individual institutions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-101)[&#8202;](#ref-back-hashtags-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-hashtags) A Thai historian scholar noted that street protests have never created political changes if the military sided with the government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc32-2)[\[3\]](#refnote-bbc32) The protests, which were organized exclusively on the academia grounds, were halted in late February due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Thailand "COVID-19 pandemic in Thailand"), with all universities, colleges, and schools shut down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc32-3)[\[3\]](#refnote-bbc32)

### Hashtags

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Hashtags") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section's source code: Hashtags")\]

The use of online media, such as TikTok and Twitter, including various [hashtags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashtag "Hashtag"), has characterized the protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-103) Hashtags have emerged for protests at each institution. For example:

- Protests at [Chulalongkorn University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chulalongkorn_University "Chulalongkorn University") used #เสาหลักจะไม่หักอีกต่อไป (lit. The pillar will no longer be broken; an analogy to the university's tagline as "the pillar of the land.")
- Protests at [Triam Udom Suksa School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triam_Udom_Suksa_School "Triam Udom Suksa School") used #เกียมอุดมไม่ก้มหัวให้เผด็จการ (lit. Giam Udom doesn't bow to dictatorship; the name "Giam Udom" is an alias of "Triam Udom")
- Protests at [Srinakharinwirot University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srinakharinwirot_University "Srinakharinwirot University") (SWU) used #มศว คนรุ่นเปลี่ยน (lit. SWU generation of change)

Some have mentioned their distaste of the pro-military and pro-monarchy conservatives (dubbed [Salim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salim_(Thai_slang) "Salim (Thai slang)") — [สลิ่ม](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B4%E0%B9%88%E0%B8%A1 "wikt:สลิ่ม"); the word was derived from Thai dessert [sarim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarim_(dessert) "Sarim (dessert)")) such as[&#8202;](#ref-back-hashtags-1)[\[93\]](#refnote-hashtags)

- Protests at [Kasetsart University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kasetsart_University "Kasetsart University") (KU) used #KUไม่ใช่ขนมหวานราดกะทิ (KU is not coconut milk dessert \[referring to [sarim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarim_(dessert) "Sarim (dessert)").\])
- Protests at [Khon Kaen University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khon_Kaen_University "Khon Kaen University") (KKU) used #KKUขอโทษที่ช้าโดนสลิ่มลบโพสต์ (KKU is sorry for being late; \[our\] posts were deleted by salims)
- Protests at [Mahidol University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahidol_University "Mahidol University") (located in [Salaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salaya,_Thailand "Salaya, Thailand")) used #ศาลายางดกินของหวานหลายสี (Salaya stops eating multi-coloured dessert \[referring to sarim.\])
- Protests at [King Mongkut's Institute of Technology Ladkrabang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Mongkut's_Institute_of_Technology_Ladkrabang "King Mongkut's Institute of Technology Ladkrabang") (Phra Chom Klao) used #พระจอมเกล้าชอบกินเหล้าไม่ชอบกินสลิ่ม (Phra chom klao loves eating \[drinking\] liquors but not salim)

## Second wave (July 2020 – December 2020)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Second wave (July 2020 – December 2020)") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section's source code: Second wave (July 2020 – December 2020)")\]

### Protests under Three Demands

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Protests under Three Demands") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section's source code: Protests under Three Demands")\]

Main article: [Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (July 2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2020_Thai_protests_(July_2020) "Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (July 2020)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%97%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%AA.jpg/250px-%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%97%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%AA.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%AA%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B5%E0%B9%80%E0%B8%97%E0%B8%A2%E0%B8%9E%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%AA.jpg) Seri Thoey group flew the [LGBT flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_flag "LGBT flag") during the protest on 25 July

On 18 July, Thailand saw the largest street demonstration since the [2014 Thai coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "2014 Thai coup d'état")[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-104) at the [Democracy Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Monument "Democracy Monument") in Bangkok with around 2,500 protesters. The protesters, organised under the name Free Youth ( [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language"): เยาวชนปลดแอก; [RTGS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_General_System_of_Transcription "Royal Thai General System of Transcription"):_yaowachon plot aek_), announced their three core demands:[&#8202;](#ref-back-cores-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-cores) dissolution of the House, ending intimidation of the people and drafting a new constitution. A Free Youth leader stated that they do not aim to overthrow the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-106) The gathering was planned to last overnight, but it was cancelled by midnight for security reasons.

After 18 July, the protests began to spread across the country. The first were in [Chiang Mai Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiang_Mai_Province "Chiang Mai Province") and [Ubon Ratchathani Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubon_Ratchathani_Province "Ubon Ratchathani Province") on 19 July.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-107) By 23 July, demonstrations had been organised in more than 20 provinces.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-108) Some major demonstrations include one in [Maha Sarakham Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maha_Sarakham_Province "Maha Sarakham Province") on 23 July, of which its hashtag #IsanSibothon quickly trended first on Twitter in Thailand,[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-109) and one in [Nakhon Ratchasima Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakhon_Ratchasima_Province "Nakhon Ratchasima Province") on 24 July saw one of the largest crowds amongst them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-110)

On 25 July, an [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT "LGBT") activist group _Seri Thoey_ (lit. Free [Thoey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kathoey "Kathoey"); parody to the _Seri Thai_ – [Free Thai Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Thai_Movement "Free Thai Movement")), demonstrated at the Democracy Monument calling for legalisation of [same-sex marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Same-sex_marriage "Same-sex marriage") in addition to the three demands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-111)

On 26 July, an event called "Let's run, [Hamtaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamtaro "Hamtaro")" was organised at the [Democracy Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Monument "Democracy Monument"). Having begun in a protest at [Triam Udom Suksa School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triam_Udom_Suksa_School "Triam Udom Suksa School") in Bangkok, but later spread on [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter"), and eventually gathered around 3,000 people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-112)

### Further demands of monarchy reform and subsequent protests

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Further demands of monarchy reform and subsequent protests") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section's source code: Further demands of monarchy reform and subsequent protests")\]

Main article: [Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (August 2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2020_Thai_protests_(August_2020) "Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (August 2020)")

Summary of Demands on Reform of the Monarchy[&#8202;](#ref-back-prachatai_11082020-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-prachatai_11082020)

> 01. Revoke the King's [immunity against lawsuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign_immunity "Sovereign immunity").
> 02. Revoke [lèse majesté law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A8se_majest%C3%A9_in_Thailand "Lèse majesté in Thailand"), give amnesty to every persecuted individual.
> 03. Separate the King's personal and royal assets.
> 04. Reduce the budget allocated to the monarchy.
> 05. Abolish the Royal Offices and unnecessary units e.g. [Privy Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Privy_Council_of_Thailand "Privy Council of Thailand").
> 06. Open assets of the monarchy to audit.
> 07. Cease the King's power to give public political comments.
> 08. Cease [propaganda around the King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_personality "Cult of personality").
> 09. Investigate the murders of commentators or critics of the monarchy.
> 10. Forbid the King to endorse future coups.

On 3 August, a [Harry Potter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Potter "Harry Potter")-themed demonstration was held, which 200 people joined, featured a public speech by [Arnon Nampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Nampa "Arnon Nampa") which openly criticised the monarchy, and demanded amendment of increasing [royal prerogative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_prerogative "Royal prerogative") and reform of the lèse majesté law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-reuters) Paul Chambers, a Southeast Asian politics scholar, noted that "Such open criticism of Thailand's monarch by non-elites at a public place within Thailand with the police simply standing by is the first of its kind in Thai history."[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters_10082020-1)[\[65\]](#refnote-reuters_10082020) The police arrested Arnon and another Free Youth leader on 8 August.

On 10 August, there was a rally at Thammasat University, Rangsit campus in [Pathum Thani Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathum_Thani_Province "Pathum Thani Province") named "ธรรมศาสตร์จะไม่ทน." (lit. Thammasat will not tolerate.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-115) by United Front of Thammasat and Demonstration group. Totaling about 3,000 people, it employed the slogan "We don't want reforms; we want revolution."[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-116) Among the events were the declaration of ten demands to reform the monarchy by [Rung Panusaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panusaya_Sithijirawattanakul "Panusaya Sithijirawattanakul"), and the monarchy reform speeches by [Panupong Jadnok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panupong_Jadnok "Panupong Jadnok") and [Arnon Nampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Nampa "Arnon Nampa").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ประชาไท-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-ประชาไท)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-:4) According to AP, the protesters at the site had mixed reaction to the demands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-119) BBC analyst called it the revolutionary speech for Thailand history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-120)

On 14 August, BBC Thai reported that there had been protests associated with Free Youth in 49 provinces, while in 11 provinces saw activism associated with pro-establishment groups.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC1408-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-BBC1408) In the same day, student activist [Parit "Penguin" Chiwarak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parit_Chiwarak "Parit Chiwarak") was arrested, leading to calls from [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") to immediately release him and drop all charges against all activists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-122)

On 16 August, a large gathering which around 20,000–25,000 people joined[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-nikkei) was held at the [Democracy Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Monument "Democracy Monument") and reiterated calls for a revised constitution and reforms to the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-124)

On 20 August, two large-scale student protests of approximately 1,000 people each were held in [Nakhon Ratchasima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakhon_Ratchasima "Nakhon Ratchasima") and [Khon Kaen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khon_Kaen "Khon Kaen"). Activists announced a "major rally" planned on 19 September 2020, at [Thammasat University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thammasat_University "Thammasat University")'s [Tha Prachan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tha_Phra_Chan "Tha Phra Chan") campus.[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-125)[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-126)[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-127)

### Formal submission of demands and responses

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Formal submission of demands and responses") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section's source code: Formal submission of demands and responses")\]

Main article: [Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (September 2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2020_Thai_protests_(September_2020) "Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (September 2020)")

On 26 August, student groups presented submissions, including the 10 demands, to the House of Representatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-128) Subsequently, the opposition and the coalition submitted constitutional amendment motions, including to amend the clause governing the constitution amendment procedure.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-130)

On 27–28 August, approximately 15,000 people gathered at [14 October Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14_October_1973_Memorial "14 October 1973 Memorial") for the first overnight protest, organized by the 'We are Friends' group.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-:3) On 28 August, as 15 anti-government activists from the 18 July rally arrived to face charges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-132)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/19_September_demonstration_3.jpg/250px-19_September_demonstration_3.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:19_September_demonstration_3.jpg) The occupation of Sanam Luang by demonstrators on 19 September 2020.

In a rally described as one of the largest protests in years,[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-133) on 19 September, protesters gathered at Thammasat University, then moved to [Sanam Luang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanam_Luang "Sanam Luang") in the afternoon and stayed overnight there, with attendance estimated at anywhere between 20,000 and 100,000,[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-134)[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-135)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-:5) On 20 September, the protesters installed a plaque symbolizing the now-missing [Khana Ratsadon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khana_Ratsadon "Khana Ratsadon") plaque at Sanam Luang.[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-137)[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-138) The protesters submitted their demands to the president of the Privy Council via the chief of the [Metropolitan Police Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_Police_Bureau_(Thailand) "Metropolitan Police Bureau (Thailand)") before dispersing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-:10) There were no reports of violence; protest leader Parit Chiwarak called for a general strike on 14 October to commemorate the [1973 Thai popular uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Thai_popular_uprising "1973 Thai popular uprising").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-1)[\[130\]](#refnote-:10) The plaque was removed less than 24 hours after it was installed;[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-140)[&#8202;](#ref-back-South_China_Morning_Post-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-South_China_Morning_Post) however, it has since proliferated as an online meme.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-142) Some international media described the rally as an open challenge to Vajiralongkorn.[&#8202;](#ref-back-South_China_Morning_Post-1)[\[132\]](#refnote-South_China_Morning_Post)[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-143)

On 24 September, Parliament voted to set up a study committee, effectively delaying a scheduled vote on constitutional amendment by at least a month. Discontent prompted #RepublicofThailand to trend first in the country's Twitter, with more than 700,000 retweets, the first mass public expression of republican sentiment in the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-144)[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-145)

### Strengthened emergency powers

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Strengthened emergency powers") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section's source code: Strengthened emergency powers")\]

Main article: [Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (October 2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2020_Thai_protests_(October_2020) "Timeline of the 2020 Thai protests (October 2020)")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/15_October_protest.jpg/250px-15_October_protest.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:15_October_protest.jpg) The 15 October protest at the [Ratchaprasong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchaprasong "Ratchaprasong") intersection.

After no major events for nearly a month, on 13 October, a small group of protesters gathered at [Ratchadamnoen Avenue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchadamnoen_Avenue "Ratchadamnoen Avenue"), to protest the passing of the king's motorcade,[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-146) resulting in the detention of activists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-:11) The planned protest rally on 14 October began at Democracy Monument, with the objective of moving to [Government House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_House_of_Thailand "Government House of Thailand") to demand the resignation of Prayut.[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-148) They were met by "counter-protesters," who were trucked in using municipal vehicles,[&#8202;](#ref-back-ks1410-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-ks1410) as well as right-wing groups, including the Thai Pakdee (Loyal Thais) and the [Rubbish Collection Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubbish_Collection_Organization "Rubbish Collection Organization"), who held their rallies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-150)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Arrests_heighten_rally_concerns-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-Arrests_heighten_rally_concerns) Later in the day, tens of thousands of protesters,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-:13) some under the umbrella of "Khana Ratsadorn 2563", were largely peaceful, marked by some violent assaults on the protesters by pro-royalists,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[61\]](#refnote-:12) marched to Government House and set up camps around it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-152) The protest coincided with a planned royal progression around the city.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-2)[\[61\]](#refnote-:12) The royal motorcade passed a protester crowd, strayed from the announced route.[&#8202;](#ref-back-khaosod_15102020-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-khaosod_15102020) Later that evening, Prayut ordered legal actions against the protesters for allegedly blocking the motorcade,[&#8202;](#ref-back-khaosod_15102020-1)[\[144\]](#refnote-khaosod_15102020) but, according to [Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reuters "Reuters"), "none appeared to be trying to reach it".[&#8202;](#ref-back-tostevin_23102020-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-tostevin_23102020) Arnon accused the authorities of intentionally arranging the motorcade through the rally site. He gave an estimate of 200,000 participants before midnight.[&#8202;](#ref-back-155-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-155)

On 15 October, the authorities declared a "severe" [state of emergency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_emergency "State of emergency") in Bangkok from 04:00 local time and banned gatherings of five or more people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ap_14102020-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-ap_14102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-157) The protesters were cleared using riot police.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ap_14102020-1)[\[147\]](#refnote-ap_14102020) In the process, police detained three protest leaders, and imposed a ban on sensitive media stories.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-158) During the day, troops were dispatched to guard the Government House and [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sappaya-Sapasathan "Sappaya-Sapasathan"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-159) raising alarm from an opposition [member of parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Member_of_parliament "Member of parliament") (MP) of resembling pre-2014 coup days.[&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-160) A smaller planned occupation of at least 13,500 participants went ahead at the [Ratchaprasong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchaprasong "Ratchaprasong") intersection,[&#8202;](#ref-back-khaosod_activists_15102020-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-khaosod_activists_15102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bkp1610-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-bkp1610)[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[j\]](#refnote-163) and more protesters were arrested.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc15Oct-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-bbc15Oct) A legal aid group reported at least 51 people were arrested between 13 and 15 October.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ks1610-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-ks1610)

[Play video](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Thai_police_disperse_Patumwan_flash_rally_16102020.webm "Play video") Duration: 7 minutes and 18 seconds.7:18Police using water cannons to disperse protesters at Pathum Wan Intersection on 16 October.

On 16 October, around 2,000 unarmed protesters, mostly teenagers, gathered at [Pathum Wan Intersection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathum_Wan_Intersection "Pathum Wan Intersection"), and two hours later were dispersed by the police. High-pressure [water cannons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_cannon "Water cannon") with chemical-filled water and [tear gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tear_gas "Tear gas") were used.[&#8202;](#ref-back-165-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-165)[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-166) The commander of the Metropolitan Police reported at least 100 people were arrested.[&#8202;](#ref-back-pcc1610-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-pcc1610) Protesters vowed to continue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bkp1610-1)[\[153\]](#refnote-bkp1610) The police defended their use of the chemicals and that they were following "international standards."[&#8202;](#ref-back-neumann_16102020-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-neumann_16102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-168)

| External videos |
| --- |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Assault of student protesters at Ramkhamhaeng University 21 October 2020](https://twitter.com/i/status/1318907110848040960), Twitter video |

Protests then organized more online and held flash protests at numerous locations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-paddock_21102020-1)[\[44\]](#refnote-paddock_21102020) There were protests almost daily from 17 to 24 October, even though they were faced with government-ordered shutdown of the capital's rapid transit systems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-169) In a televised speech on 21 October, Prayut suggested that both sides reconcile their differences through the parliamentary process.[&#8202;](#ref-back-paddock_21102020-2)[\[44\]](#refnote-paddock_21102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bp_22102020-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-bp_22102020) On the same day, a group of royalist counter-protesters wearing yellow shirts assaulted the protesters at Ramkhamhaeng University,[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-171) resulting in one injured student.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mgr2210-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-mgr2210) The following day, Prayut revoked the severe emergency declaration, on the grounds that the severity of the situation had lessened.[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-172)[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-173)

Prayut scheduled an emergency parliamentary session for 26–27 October.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-174) Protesters, however, preferred that the government show good faith and resign before advocating for amendments.[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-175) The non-voting sessions addressed none of the protesters' concerns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cna_27102020-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-cna_27102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-177-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-177) As a result, Prayut said that the government would present a Constitutional amendment bill and a reconciliation committee to resolve the political conflict would be set up.[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-178)[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-179)

### Further anti-royal protests

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Further anti-royal protests") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section's source code: Further anti-royal protests")\]

Main article: [Timeline of the 2020–2021 Thai protests (November 2020)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests_(November_2020) "Timeline of the 2020–2021 Thai protests (November 2020)")

On 26 October, protesters marched to the [German Embassy in Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_Germany,_Bangkok "Embassy of Germany, Bangkok"), petitioning the German government to investigate the King's activities in Germany, if he had exercising powers from German soil.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cna_27102020-1)[\[166\]](#refnote-cna_27102020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-180)[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-181) And on 3 November, a demonstration was held to protest a block of the [Pornhub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pornhub "Pornhub") website, a move suspected to restrict access to some compromising material of the king.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-182)[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-183) Meanwhile, on 1 November, a group of yellow-shirted royalists demonstrated their support for the King at the Grand Palace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-184)

On 8 November, an estimated 7,000–10,000 protesters marched from Democracy Monument to the [Grand Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Palace "Grand Palace") to deliver their letters to the King.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kuhakan_08112020-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-kuhakan_08112020) The protesters insisted that their demand to reform the monarchy is already the best compromise they could offer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-186) The gathering was largely peaceful, but the 9,000 men strong police[&#8202;](#ref-back-cna_08112020-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-cna_08112020) employed water cannons for the second time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kuhakan_08112020-1)[\[175\]](#refnote-kuhakan_08112020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP0811-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-AP0811) Though brief, the incident caused five injuries, including one police officer, according to the capital's emergency medical center.[&#8202;](#ref-back-mtc0811-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-mtc0811)

On 14 November, around 20 protesting groups ranging from high school, women rights to LGBTQ activists in an event called "Mob Fest". One event resulted in a covering of Democracy Monument with cloth, and a small clash with the police.[&#8202;](#ref-back-189-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-189)

On 17 November, the Senate and House of Representatives began a two-day joint session to consider changes to the constitution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yuda_18112020-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-yuda_18112020) That day, at least 55 people were hurt when protesters near Parliament clashed with the police and yellow-shirted royalists. Police fired tear gas and water cannons at the crowd. Six people suffered gunshot wounds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-yuda_18112020-1)[\[22\]](#refnote-yuda_18112020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-190) On the second day, lawmakers rejected five of the seven proposals to amend the constitution, including the submission by Internet Law Reform Dialogue, or iLaw, which was most preferred by the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-191)

On 18 November, angered by the rejection of the people-proposed constitutional bill and the use of force the day before, thousands of protesters gathered at the [Royal Thai Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Police "Royal Thai Police")'s headquarters and hurled paint and sprayed graffiti in the area.[&#8202;](#ref-back-192-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-192) On 19 November, police officers and pro-government volunteers rushed to clean it, which earned a thanks from Prayut.[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-193) On 21 November, high school students led thousands of protesters in Bangkok. In addition to the common protest themes of government and monarchy reform, high school students are seeking more freedom and fairness in an education system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-194)[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-195)

| External videos |
| --- |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Clip of unknown person throwing explosive at rally 25 November 2020](https://twitter.com/i/status/1331632174236241920), Twitter video |

On 25 November, over 10,000 protesters converged on the headquarters of [Siam Commercial Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siam_Commercial_Bank "Siam Commercial Bank") (SCB) in northern Bangkok, in which the King is the largest shareholder,[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei_25112020-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-nikkei_25112020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-197) to demand an investigation into the king's wealth and spending. The police had heavily barricaded the area with shipping containers, concrete barricades and razor wire. The day before, Thai authorities ordered 12 protest leaders to turn themselves in on 1 December and face charges that include lèse-majesté.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei_25112020-1)[\[186\]](#refnote-nikkei_25112020)[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-198) A well-known Thai royalist scholar [Sulak Sivaraksa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulak_Sivaraksa "Sulak Sivaraksa") decried Prayut's using the lèse-majesté law and called for the prime minister's removal from office.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-199) At least two protesters were wounded in an explosion and shooting incident late into the night, four or five shots being fired.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-200)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei_1015-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-nikkei_1015) The police put the blame on rivalry in the vocational students group themselves.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-202)

On 27 November, about 5,000 protesters joined an anti-coup drill in northern Bangkok's [Lat Phrao Intersection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lat_Phrao_Intersection "Lat Phrao Intersection"). Protest leader [Panupong "Mike Rayong" Jadnok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panupong_%22Mike_Rayong%22_Jadnok "Panupong \"Mike Rayong\" Jadnok") urged people to park vehicles at key Bangkok intersections in the event of a coup, to obstruct military movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei_1015-1)[\[191\]](#refnote-nikkei_1015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-203)

On 29 November, thousands of protesters marched to a barracks, demanding the King to give up control of some army regiments. At the [11th Infantry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_Infantry_Regiment_(Thailand) "11th Infantry Regiment (Thailand)"), one of two army units that the king brought under his direct command in 2019, protesters splashed red paint on the ground, referencing the deadly army crackdown on anti-government redshirt demonstrators in 2010. They were blocked at the gate by riot police in gas masks and helmets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-204)[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-205)

On 2 December, the Constitutional Court ruled in favor of Prayut in a conflict-of-interest case over his use of military housing. The former army chief had been living in a military residence after retiring from the army in 2014, months after he led the coup over the elected government. The ruling allowed Pruyut to remain in power. Thousands of protested gathered at the Lat Phrao Intersection to protest the verdict.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-207)

On 7 December, Free Youth began a Restart Thailand campaign emphasizing the importance of farmers and laborers. Their use of a [hammer and sickle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammer_and_sickle "Hammer and sickle") banner drew criticism for its links to [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism"). Many student leaders distanced themselves from the group, concerned that new protesters would be discouraged.[&#8202;](#ref-back-na_23122020-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-na_23122020)

On 10 December, democracy activists rallied at the UN's Bangkok office, held up a banner saying "Repeal Lèse-Majesté Law." Representatives were admitted to the building to hand over a letter, requesting the UN to pressure the Thai government into repealing Lèse-majesté laws they say are being used to suppress their movement. Hundreds of protesters also rallied at the 14 October 1973 Memorial that commemorates the lives of pro-democracy supporters lost during a [military massacre in 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_Thai_popular_uprising "1973 Thai popular uprising"), they raised the three-finger salute and shouted "Abolish 112," referring to the criminal code of lèse-majesté law. Ahead of the rally, protest leaders held a news conference at the 14 October 1973 Memorial. "We join together to demand the abolishment of this legal provision," a protest leader said, reading out a prepared statement regarding lèse-majesté in English.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-209)[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-210)

After five months of street protest, the movement quieted in late December before New Year, which also coincided with a COVID-19 outbreak in the country. Protest leaders said they were taking a "break".[&#8202;](#ref-back-na_23122020-1)[\[198\]](#refnote-na_23122020)

## Third wave (February 2021 – April 2021)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Third wave (February 2021 – April 2021)") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section's source code: Third wave (February 2021 – April 2021)")\]

Protestors took a break in December 2020 and January 2021 as Thailand was hit by a second wave of [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19 "COVID-19") infections during the pandemic. During the break from street protests, the movement continued to voice their opinions online, while prominent members faced legal battles. Between November and December 2020, 38 individuals were charged with lese-majeste.[&#8202;](#ref-back-tpbs150121-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-tpbs150121) A student was arrested at his dormitory at 3.00 a.m. on 14 January 2021, leading to a flash protest at the local police station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-212)

On 16 January 2021, five protesters at a flash protest targeted at lese-majeste law were arrested by anti-riot police, marking the first street protest in 2021. A small explosion from a ping-pong bomb was heard which was apparently thrown at the approaching police, raising concerns of increasing violence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-213) A member of a protest security group was kidnapped on the same day and was later found on 17 January, which state security personnel were thought to be the perpetuator.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-214)

On 19 January 2021, [Benja Apan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benja_Apan "Benja Apan") from Thammasat group went to [Iconsiam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iconsiam "Iconsiam") shopping mall owned by [Sirindhorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sirindhorn "Sirindhorn") and [Charoen Pokphand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charoen_Pokphand "Charoen Pokphand") group to protest holding signs saying "Monopolizing the vaccine to give the spotlight to the monarchy", directly linked to [Vajiralongkorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajiralongkorn "Vajiralongkorn")'s [Siam Bioscience](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siam_Bioscience "Siam Bioscience") drug company. She was harassed and assaulted by Iconsiam guard.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-215)[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-216)

| External videos |
| --- |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Police brutality on man dressed as medic 13 Feb 2021](https://twitter.com/i/status/1360642452059615235), Twitter video |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Police chasing fleeing protesters 20 Mar 2021](https://twitter.com/i/status/1373337783595532288), Twitter video |

On 1 February, in response to [2021 Myanmar coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Myanmar_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "2021 Myanmar coup d'état"), Thai and Myanmar protesters organised an event at the Myanmar embassy in Bangkok, resulting in three arrests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-217) On 10 February, the protesters held the first street rally in months, to protest the detention of four prominent members who were charged with lese majeste, among others. In the evening, explosive sound was heard, and an empty tear gas canister was found. The police, without any investigation, readily told reporters that the canister was stolen from prior rallies. Ten activists were briefly detained for hanging protest banners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-218)[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-219)

On 13 February, the protesters held a peaceful demonstration at Democracy Monument and the City Pillar Shrine. However, clashes between them and the police happened in the evening, with video of riot police stomping a men dressed in a medic uniform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-220) Eleven more activists were detained,[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-221) and #ตำรวจกระทืบหมอ ("police stomped doctor") trended the country's Twitter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-222)

On 28 February, as the movement was trying to rebuild momentum since the break and jailing of some core protesters,[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-223) the Restart Democracy group (rebranded from Free Youth group) held an event at [1st Infantry Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1st_Infantry_Regiment_(Thailand) "1st Infantry Regiment (Thailand)"), where Prayut's residence and the headquarter of King's Close Bodyguard are located. The skirmishes between both sides began in the evening, shortly before a decision to disperse. Some hardline protesters held their ground, while throwing objects at the police. The police retaliated by firing water cannons, tear gas, and rubber bullets at them. According to Bangkok's emergency medical service, 10 protesters and 22 police officers were injured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ke1Mar-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-ke1Mar) The leaderless tactics employed there were criticized by fellow protest organizers, who pointed out the communication problems and inability to deescalate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ke1Mar-1)[\[214\]](#refnote-ke1Mar) During the event, out of uniform security personnel were seen near the protest venue, the second time since the 13 February event.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-225)

On 17 March, Thai Parliament voted down two constitutional amendment bills,[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-226) after the Constitutional Court ruled that the amendment must pass a referendum first.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-227)

On 20 March, a protester group called REDEM, amounting about 1,000, gathered at Sanam Luang, in front of the [Grand Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Palace "Grand Palace") in Bangkok. The venue was heavily barricaded with shipping containers. The skirmish broke out when some protesters tried to remove the barricade, but they were met with police water cannons, tear gas and rubber bullets. Some protesters used molotov cocktails and burned tires. One journalist opined that the government response was intensified compared to last year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-228)[&#8202;](#ref-back-229-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-229)[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-230) 33 people, including 12 police officers, were reported injured, and 32 people were arrested.[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-231)

On 29 April, [Benja Apan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benja_Apan "Benja Apan"), Nutchanon Pairoj, and a group of other students came to the [Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_Court_of_Thailand "Criminal Court of Thailand") to send an open letter demanding the release of imprisoned activists. They called Chanathip Muanpawong, the judge who denied bail, to come to receive the letter. Benja scattered paper printed with the names of 11,035 supporters on the stairs to the court building, and said that the students are not a threat to national security but wanting to improve Thai society and for the monarchy to exist under the law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-232-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-232)

## Fourth wave (May 2021 – November 2021)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Fourth wave (May 2021 – November 2021)") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section's source code: Fourth wave (May 2021 – November 2021)")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Thai_Riot_Police_Dindang_Protest.JPG/330px-Thai_Riot_Police_Dindang_Protest.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Thai_Riot_Police_Dindang_Protest.JPG) [Riot Police](https://th.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%AD%E0%B8%B2%E0%B8%A3%E0%B8%B1%E0%B8%81%E0%B8%82%E0%B8%B2%E0%B9%81%E0%B8%A5%E0%B8%B0%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%A7%E0%B8%9A%E0%B8%84%E0%B8%B8%E0%B8%A1%E0%B8%9D%E0%B8%B9%E0%B8%87%E0%B8%8A%E0%B8%99 "th:กองบังคับการอารักขาและควบคุมฝูงชน") fire tear gas to anti-government protesters during Din Daeng Protests in August 2021

| External image |
| --- |
| [![image icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/6/61/Searchtool.svg/20px-Searchtool.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Searchtool.svg)[Police pointing firearm at unarmed civilian 1 Aug 2021](https://www.facebook.com/VoiceOnlineTH/photos/10162018947389848), Facebook |

Although the new NGO laws had been implemented\[ _[_non sequitur_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] and the pandemic was still serious, starting from mid-June, a new wave of protest started.[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-233) Further protests were arranged in July,[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-234) with police employing tear gas and rubber bullets against some of the protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-235)[&#8202;](#ref-back-236-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-236)

Rallies occurred in more than 30 provinces on 1 August.[&#8202;](#ref-back-237-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-237)

On 3 August, [Arnon Nampa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Nampa "Arnon Nampa") gave a critical speech once again on the front of [Bangkok Art and Culture Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok_Art_and_Culture_Centre "Bangkok Art and Culture Centre") to commemorate 'Harry Potter themed' last year. This time he demanded the revocation of Article 112 and transferring of public assets from the King back to the previous status.[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-238) He has been in jail since 9 August until today on [lese majeste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lese_majeste "Lese majeste") charges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-239-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-239)

On 7 August, the Free Youth group planned a march to the [Grand Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Palace "Grand Palace") from the [Democracy Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Monument "Democracy Monument") asking for reform and a good vaccine program, but Thai riot police pressured them out before the event starts. They headed back to the parliament. But later, they went further to the [Victory Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Monument_(Thailand) "Victory Monument (Thailand)"), preparing to go to Prayut Chan-o-cha house. Thai riot police closed the [Din Daeng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Din_Daeng "Din Daeng") area to prevent protesters from going near Prayuth's house. The police fired a lot of tear gas at them.
