It was the beginning of Din Daeng's later series of protests. Around 6pm, the police truck was burned near the Victory Monument. Din Daeng's non-organized protest, called 'Talugaz', started on this day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-240-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-240)[&#8202;](#ref-back-241-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-241)

On 10 August, UFTD 'Car Mob' led by [Benja Apan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benja_Apan "Benja Apan") protest which started at [Ratchaprasong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchaprasong "Ratchaprasong") intersection. She stated in front of the Sino-Thai office, a construction company owned by Deputy Prime Minister [Anutin Charnvirakul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anutin_Charnvirakul "Anutin Charnvirakul"), that the [2014 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Thai_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "2014 Thai coup d'état") by Prime Minister [Prayut Chan-o-cha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prayut_Chan-o-cha "Prayut Chan-o-cha") benefited only the elite. She criticized the government's mishandling of the COVID-19 pandemic, reboot the economy, repeals the junta constitution, reforms in state structures, and the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-242)

On 16 August, in series of street fight around Din Deang area, [Warit Somnoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warit_Somnoi?action=edit&redlink=1 "Warit Somnoi (page does not exist)"), a teenager, was shot in the head in front of the Din Daeng police station. The bullet came from the police station. He was hospitalized in coma.[&#8202;](#ref-back-warit-1)[\[40\]](#refnote-warit)

On 7 October, a policeman was shot in the head in the Din Daeng area. The police raided the flats, held 13 people for questioning (7 of them younger than 13 years of age) and reported that it had seized many weapons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-243)

On 28 October, after 2-month coma, Warit Somnoi died.[&#8202;](#ref-back-warit-2)[\[40\]](#refnote-warit) On the next day, pro-democracy supporters gathered at the Din Daeng police station to hold a candlelit vigil. The police pushed them back and arrested 2 people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-244-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-244)

On 31 October, Panusaya called for the lese majeste law to be repealed in Ratchaprasong area. There were more than 3,000 people gathering.[&#8202;](#ref-back-245-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-245)[&#8202;](#ref-back-246-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-246) Panusaya cut her left arm open with the message '112' in the end of her speech.[&#8202;](#ref-back-247-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-247)

By 2022, street protests largely died down, which human rights groups have attributed to heavy suppression and surveillance by the Thai government, although activism has continued online due to being more difficult to prosecute.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto2-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-auto2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-auto1)

## Financing

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=15 "Edit section: Financing") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section's source code: Financing")\]

The protests are financed by private donations,[&#8202;](#ref-back-248-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-248)[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-249) mainly from actress [Intira Charoenpura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intira_Charoenpura "Intira Charoenpura")[&#8202;](#ref-back-250-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-250)[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-251) and the [K-pop fandom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fandom_culture_in_South_Korea "Fandom culture in South Korea") in Thailand[&#8202;](#ref-back-252-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-253-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-253) — the latter alone donated over [฿](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_baht "Thai baht") 3,600,000 (around [US$](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_dollar "United States dollar") 115,399) on 18 October 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-254-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-254) There have been attempts to prosecute the donors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-255) A conspiracy theory accusing foreigners, including the United States government and American organisations such as [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") and [Netflix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netflix "Netflix"), of funding the protests has been voiced by the royalist group Thailand Vision and campaigners at pro-government rallies leading the [U.S. embassy in Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embassy_of_the_United_States,_Bangkok "Embassy of the United States, Bangkok") to issue a formal statement of denial.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-256) Pro-monarchy demonstrators gathered at the embassy on 27 October, demanding that the United States end the " [hybrid war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_war "Hybrid war")" against Thailand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-257)[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-258)

## Reactions

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=16 "Edit section: Reactions") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section's source code: Reactions")\]

Main article: [Reactions to the 2020 Thai protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2020_Thai_protests "Reactions to the 2020 Thai protests")

### State response and arrests

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=17 "Edit section: State response and arrests") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section's source code: State response and arrests")\]

| External videos |
| --- |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Arrest of student leader Parit Chiwarak on 14 August 2020](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=EOvrSMNNWlk) (3:09, in Thai), YouTube video. |
| [![video icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg/20px-Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Nuvola_apps_kaboodle.svg)[Arrest of student leader Pai Daodin on 13 October 2020](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=r4eE7Z_WpAY) (0:39), Youtube video. |

An _Isaan Record_ analysis found that the government response has included force and [intimidation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intimidation "Intimidation"), [arbitrary detention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitrary_detention "Arbitrary detention"), arrests and changes, disinformation, the deployment of [military Information and Operations units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Thai_Army#Information_operation "Royal Thai Army"), media [censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship "Censorship"), [delaying tactics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaying_tactic "Delaying tactic"), [obfuscation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obfuscation "Obfuscation"), support for pro-government groups, [gaslighting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaslighting "Gaslighting"), and negotiation,[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-259) as well as mobile jamming devices.[&#8202;](#ref-back-260-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-260) The Government Public Relations Department of Thailand released two propaganda YouTube videos attacking the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-261-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-261)

Prayut blamed the protesters for further damaging the country's economy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-262) Key military personnel, such as General [Apirat Kongsompong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apirat_Kongsompong "Apirat Kongsompong"), the [Commander-in-Chief of the Royal Thai Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_commanders-in-chief_of_the_Royal_Thai_Army "List of commanders-in-chief of the Royal Thai Army"), condemned the protesters, even accusing them of lése majesté,[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-263) while the new Army commander Narongpan Jittkaewtae told the protesters to "reform yourselves first."[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-264) Public Health Minister [Anutin Charnvirakul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anutin_Charnvirakul "Anutin Charnvirakul"), a notable politician from a junior coalition partner, expressed concern about implications for COVID-19 spread whilst signaling his own neutrality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-265) However, the country's coronavirus caseloads remained low amidst the growing protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-266)

The authorities have intimidated protesters via the state academic system. The Thai authorities, referencing the [Thammasat University Massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thammasat_University_massacre "Thammasat University massacre") and [1992 protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_May_(1992) "Black May (1992)"), ordered universities to prevent students from demanding monarchy reform and to compile lists of protest leaders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-267) Some universities and schools responded by forbidding their staff and students to join the protests and by banning gatherings on their grounds, claiming COVID-19 concerns, while police issued warning letters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-268) On 18 August, a Ministry of Education department allowed students to hold rallies in state schools.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-269) However, in late August, student groups reported that at least 109 schools had suppressed or intimidated political expression.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-270)

As of December 2020[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit), at least 234 people were arrested, with charges including [sedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedition "Sedition");[&#8202;](#ref-back-tpbs150121-1)[\[201\]](#refnote-tpbs150121) five were arrested without charges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-271) In early August, watchdog organization iLaw reported at least 78 incidents of intimidation of protest sympathizers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BBC1408-1)[\[112\]](#refnote-BBC1408) In early September, the police summonsed the first schoolchild protester for violating the security laws.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-272) At least 63 protesters have been charged under the controversial and repeatedly extended COVID-19 emergency decree, in 17 cases, despite the government claiming it would not be employed in this way.[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-273) The people in police custody are sometimes found injured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bkp3110-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-bkp3110)

The state has attempted to severely restrict online freedom of speech. On 24 August, in response to a Thai government order, [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook,_Inc. "Facebook, Inc.") blocked access in Thailand to the million-member main [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") page critical of the monarchy, Royalist Marketplace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-274-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-274) Facebook is challenging the order in the courts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-275-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-275)
The authorities attempted to block more than 2,200 websites ahead of the 19 September rally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-276) Following the rally, a minister filed a complaint to prosecute Facebook, Twitter, and YouTube for allowing anti-government content, the first time the Computer Crimes Act has been used against foreign service providers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-277) A Thai media provider was reported to be censoring a foreign news network reporting the protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-278)

A severe state of emergency was declared in Bangkok during 15–22 October,[&#8202;](#ref-back-asianik1510-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-asianik1510) during which the police moved to ban or block anti-government or independent media, together with the Free Youth Facebook page,[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-280) and seized books criticizing the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-281) [Ministry of Digital Economy and Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Digital_Economy_and_Society "Ministry of Digital Economy and Society") stated it intended to prosecute internet service providers and online platforms which allows prohibited content, and reported about 320,000 illegal messages.[&#8202;](#ref-back-282-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-282) In response, several Thai press associations issued a joint statement opposing suppression of the media.[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-283) The authorities also required foreign diplomats to obtain advance permission to observe protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-284) An extraordinary parliamentary session was held 26–27 October to attempt to resolve the political situation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-285-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-285)

[Bangkok Metropolitan Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok_Metropolitan_Administration "Bangkok Metropolitan Administration") lend its support to the counter-protesters by transporting them to the rally site,[&#8202;](#ref-back-ks1410-1)[\[140\]](#refnote-ks1410) as well as accommodating them with trash trucks and mobile toilets.[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-286)

Police crackdowns were also more frequent in 2021, seeing the use of batons, tear gas and water cannons. The police were also accused of torture and using live rounds,[&#8202;](#ref-back-287-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-287) with the first protester died in October 2021 after being shot by a person inside a police station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-warit-3)[\[40\]](#refnote-warit) Several prominent protesters were also facing " [lawfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawfare "Lawfare")" from the authorities. In many occasions, those charged with lèse-majesté were denied bail and [detained before trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remand_(detention) "Remand (detention)"), and many of them performed hunger strikes to call for their right of getting bail.[&#8202;](#ref-back-288-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-288)

#### The Constitutional Court verdict

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=18 "Edit section: The Constitutional Court verdict") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section's source code: The Constitutional Court verdict")\]

> "The actions have hidden intentions to overthrow the constitutional monarchy and were not a call for reform,"

—A constitutional court judge, 2021[&#8202;](#ref-back-reutersnovember-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-reutersnovember)

On 10 November 2021, the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand "Constitutional Court of Thailand") ruled that [Arnon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arnon_Nampa "Arnon Nampa"), [Panupong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panupong_Jadnok "Panupong Jadnok"), and [Panusaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panusaya_Sithijirawattanakul "Panusaya Sithijirawattanakul") of the 10-point call for reforms of the monarchy in 'Thammasat will not tolerate' rally on 10 August 2020 aimed to overthrow the state and the monarchy in their speeches.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reutersnovember-1)[\[279\]](#refnote-reutersnovember) The court ordered them and other protest groups to end all monarchy reform movements. The petition was filed by Natthaporn Toprayoon on 3 September 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-290-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-290)

Without examining the evidence submitted by the defendants, the court explained that such demands were an "abuse of the rights and freedoms and harmed the state's security", but the court did not enforce a punishment on them, only ruling on the constitutionality of their demands. Three protest leaders deny seeking to overthrow the monarchy, with their lawyer and Panusaya walked out of the middle of hearing. Sunai Phasuk, a senior researcher with Human Rights Watch, labelled the ruling as "a judicial coup" that could pave the way for further legal cases against protesters, including treason. The court also asserted that sovereign power belongs to the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardiannovember-1)[\[42\]](#refnote-guardiannovember)

### The King's position

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=19 "Edit section: The King's position") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section's source code: The King's position")\]

The King and the palace initially did not express any public opinion about the protests. In August, the [Asia Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Times "Asia Times") quoted a government official stating that the King was not bothered by the "children's" protests,[&#8202;](#ref-back-291-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-291) but an [Al Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Jazeera_Media_Network "Al Jazeera Media Network") article reported that the Thai monarchy has asked Thai media to censor mention of the ten demands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-292-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-292)

On 16 October 2020, a speech by Vajiralongkorn the day before was made public, stating: "Now it is understood that the country needs people who love the country and love the monarchy".[&#8202;](#ref-back-beech_16102020-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-beech_16102020) On 24 October, a video of the King praising former PDRC leader and ex-monk Buddha Issara and another counter-protester who raised a picture of late King [Bhumibol Adulyadej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhumibol "Bhumibol") during a protest was posted to a royalist Facebook group;[&#8202;](#ref-back-294-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-294) royalists embraced it, while the protesters viewed the comment as a position statement, prompting the hashtag #23ตุลาตาสว่าง (23Oct Eyes Opened) to trend on the country's Twitter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-295-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-295) He made the first public comment in November 2020: that he "love\[&#8202;](#ref-back-296-0)[s\] them \[Thai people\] all the same," and "Thailand is the land of compromise."[\[286\]](#refnote-296)

Patrick Jory, a senior lecturer in Southeast Asian history at the University of Queensland, described the unpredictable nature of the King and "his willingness to use violence," and says that he may have pressured Prayut to suppress the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-297)

### Support

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Support") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section's source code: Support")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/Pride_flag_in_Bangkok_protest_2020_01.jpg/250px-Pride_flag_in_Bangkok_protest_2020_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Pride_flag_in_Bangkok_protest_2020_01.jpg) Protesters in Bangkok holding an [LGBT pride flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_pride_flag "LGBT pride flag")

In August 2020, a [Move Forward Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Move_Forward_Party "Move Forward Party") MP stated that some references to the monarchy in the protests were inconvenient truths that required attention. He received stark responses from _Manager Online_ and General [Apirat Kongsompong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apirat_Kongsompong "Apirat Kongsompong"), who insisted that some protesters intended to overthrow the monarchy, or had fallen victim to third-party manipulators.[&#8202;](#ref-back-298-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-298) In early September, the leader of the [Pheu Thai Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheu_Thai_Party "Pheu Thai Party"), [Sompong Amornwiwat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sompong_Amornwiwat "Sompong Amornwiwat"), stated a motion from the opposition coalition to amend Article 256 was aligned with the demands of the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-299-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-299)

Despite threats to their careers,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-:7) many Thai celebrities have publicly expressed support for the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-301-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-301)[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-302)[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-303)[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-304) A group of 147 university faculty members has issued a statement saying the protesters' demand for monarchical reform did not violate the law. The Thai Academic Network for Civil Rights, another group of 358 scholars, has also expressed full support for the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nikkei-1)[\[114\]](#refnote-nikkei) A physician was sacked for signing petition in support of the movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-305)

In August 2020, [UNICEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNICEF "UNICEF") issued a statement invoking the [Convention on the Rights of the Child](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Rights_of_the_Child "Convention on the Rights of the Child") that called for schools and learning institutions to be safe havens and forums for children's freedom of expression.[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-306)[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-307) [Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Human_Rights "Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights") and [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International") recognized the peaceful nature of the rallies, and condemned police crackdowns.[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-308)[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-309) The [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") Asia director stated, "Criminalizing peaceful protests and calls for political reform is a hallmark of authoritarian rule" and called for governments and the United Nations to condemn the repression of the protests and urge the release of protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bkp1610-2)[\[153\]](#refnote-bkp1610) Some international groups and individuals expressed their support for the movements, for example Hong Kong student protesters,[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-310) including activist [Joshua Wong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joshua_Wong "Joshua Wong").[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-311) Civil society groups in South Korea urged their government to stop exporting water cannons to Thailand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-312) UN human rights experts, including [Clément Nyaletsossi Voule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cl%C3%A9ment_Nyaletsossi_Voule "Clément Nyaletsossi Voule"), [Special Rapporteur on the Right to Freedom of Peaceful Assembly and of Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Special_Rapporteur_on_the_Rights_to_Freedom_of_Peaceful_Assembly_and_of_Association "United Nations Special Rapporteur on the Rights to Freedom of Peaceful Assembly and of Association"), issued a statement urging the government to guarantee the fundamental rights of free speech and peaceful assembly and to remove the ban on peaceful protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-313)

In October 2020, _Khaosod English_ and _Bangkok Post_ editorials called for Prayut to resign, but both did not press the demands to draft a new constitution and reform the monarchy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-314)[&#8202;](#ref-back-315-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-315)

### Opposition

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Opposition") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section's source code: Opposition")\]

State-sponsored[&#8202;](#ref-back-316-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-316) organized opposition to the protests emerged in August and included the accusation of a global conspiracy being funded or masterminded by a foreign government or NGOs. On 10 August, the Thai Move Institute released a diagram of an alleged 'people's revolution network' linking student protests to former FFP leader [Thanathorn Juangroongruangkit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thanathorn_Juangroongruangkit "Thanathorn Juangroongruangkit") and former prime minister [Thaksin Shinawatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thaksin_Shinawatra "Thaksin Shinawatra").[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-317)[&#8202;](#ref-back-318-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-318) The police and right-wing groups targeted Thanatorn and his allies for being accused of masterminding the protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-319-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-319)[&#8202;](#ref-back-320-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-320) A singer and protester posted a video clip revealing a protester shot with live bullet by the counter-protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-321-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-321) Groups of males with very short haircut and similar clothing are speculated to be out-of-uniform military or police officers who staged counter protests.[&#8202;](#ref-back-322-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-322)

Right-wing media and organizations quickly attacked the August demands for monarchy reform, stating that the call to reform the monarchy was an act of evil, causing disunity and undermining the monarchy, and accusing the [Pheu Thai Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pheu_Thai_Party "Pheu Thai Party") and the former FFP of manipulating protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-323-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-323) Most politicians expressed a negative reaction to the demands, including [Sudarat Keyuraphan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudarat_Keyuraphan "Sudarat Keyuraphan"), an influential Pheu Thai politician.[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-324) On social media platforms, user accounts, including ones coordinated by the ultra-royalist [Rubbish Collection Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubbish_Collection_Organization "Rubbish Collection Organization"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-325) which has been characterized as [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist "Fascist"), attacked protesters,[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[k\]](#refnote-329) some going so far as to urging [child rape](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_sexual_abuse "Child sexual abuse").[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-330) In mid-September, Thailand's [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand "Constitutional Court of Thailand") accepted a complaint of [treason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treason "Treason") against the 10 Demands protest leaders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-331)[&#8202;](#ref-back-332-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-332) Right-wing groups and media quickly attacked the protesters from the royal motorcade incident on 14 October.[&#8202;](#ref-back-333-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-333)[&#8202;](#ref-back-334-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-334)

At least 103 cases of harassment of students have been reported,[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-335) and protesting tactics have been condemned; some considered a protester's act of hurling paint at police officers to be violent or not peaceful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-336)[&#8202;](#ref-back-337-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-337)[&#8202;](#ref-back-338-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-338) Opponents also find vulgar language used by protest leaders unacceptable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-339)

On 28 November 2020, [Warong Dechgitvigrom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warong_Dechgitvigrom "Warong Dechgitvigrom"), a right-wing physician formed the new right wing group " [Thai Pakdee Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_Pakdee_Party "Thai Pakdee Party")" ( [Thai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language "Thai language"): พรรคไทยภักดี; lit. Loyal Thai), he also
attacking the protester and supporting the government and the monarchy which he reacted to [Jonathan Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Head "Jonathan Head"), [BBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News "BBC News") journalist during an interview in Bangkok[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-340) he said that he believes if English people used the words against Queen Elizabeth, they would have been jailed a long time ago.

Royalist and pro-government media often ran stories which portrayed counter-protesters as victims, or the protesters as agitating for violence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-341)[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-342) Royalists believe that protesters want the monarchy dissolved, a position which they have denied.[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-343) Some business known for supporting the regime, such as [Foodpanda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foodpanda "Foodpanda"), issued a statement branding the movement "terrorists," prompting consumer boycott.[&#8202;](#ref-back-344-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-344)

### Polls

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Polls") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section's source code: Polls")\]

A national Suan Dusit poll from 16 to 21 August 2020 of 197,029 people found that 59.1% stated the students were making demands as permitted in a democracy, 62.8% agreed with the demand for reform of the Constitution and 53.9% agreed that the Prime Minister Prayut Chan-o-cha should "resign or dissolve parliament", while 59.5% agreed that the government should "stop intimidating" people. Overall support for the protests stood at 53.7%, with 41.2% opposed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-345-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-345)

## Analysis

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Analysis") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section's source code: Analysis")\]

### Protestor tactics and demands

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Protestor tactics and demands") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section's source code: Protestor tactics and demands")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Bangkok_Protests_15_October_2020_Ratchaprasong_Bangkok_06.jpg/250px-Bangkok_Protests_15_October_2020_Ratchaprasong_Bangkok_06.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bangkok_Protests_15_October_2020_Ratchaprasong_Bangkok_06.jpg) Protesters on 15 October at [Ratchaprasong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ratchaprasong "Ratchaprasong") calling for reform and the release of their friends.

Some commentators say demands for monarchy reform before have only been made by fringe groups, yet there is consensus that this protest is the first time such demands have been discussed publicly. The calls may drive away some sympathisers, but if the government cracks down on the protests as a consequence, it could bring more support to the movement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-346) In mid-August, Atith Keating wrote that student protests lack a coherent strategy; they do not have plans beyond day-to-day demonstrations. This may be caused by the lack of a centralised structure, as in the peak of student movements in the 1970s.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-347) Student protesters are seen as being more creative, networked, and technologically savvy than the government and as having time on their side, as well as being the targets of disproportionate persecution that could sway public opinion, with at least a chance of causing the government to collapse provided the anti-monarchy element does not provoke a backlash.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-348) One _Bangkok Post_ columnist wrote that [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism") sentiment has never been stronger in Thailand,[&#8202;](#ref-back-349-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-349) while another opined that the 19 September 2020 protest was a [critical juncture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Path_dependence "Path dependence") for the movement, with the protesters needing to broaden their agenda again to wider societal reforms if it were to succeed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[127\]](#refnote-:5) A political scientist professor opined that an absence of protest leaders increased the risk of the movement getting out of control and could further no strategic initiatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-350)

The protest movement's utilization of coordinated protest techniques, including the use of communications technology, memes, music and social media has contrasted with the traditional techniques used by law enforcement, such as police battalions, state propaganda, and water cannons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-:6) After the police crackdown on 16 October 2020, the hashtag #WhatsHappeningInThailand was created and heavily utilized, with content published in various languages across various social media platforms in order to attract global attention to the situation in the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-352-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-352)

In some rallies, most of the participants were women, including schoolgirls protesting for social change and against [gender inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_inequality "Gender inequality") and [patriarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarchy "Patriarchy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-353-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-353)

After the arrest of many of Thai protest leaders, following [their counterparts in Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Hong_Kong_protests "2019–20 Hong Kong protests"), activists in Thailand changed their tactics. Claiming themselves to all be leaders, tactics such as holding up umbrellas in order to shield themselves from tear gas canisters have been used.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-354) According to AP, the protesters increasingly emphasize on the reform of the monarchy, but faced serious obstacles, including, notably, opposition party leaders who agreed with all their other demands.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP0811-1)[\[178\]](#refnote-AP0811) [Thitinan Pongsudhirak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thitinan_Pongsudhirak "Thitinan Pongsudhirak"), a political science professor from Chulalongkorn University, opined that the strength of the movement has been decreasing since the jailing of core leaders and the introduction of the hammer and sickle symbol in late 2020, and criticized the movement for being too ambitious and employing counterproductive tactics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-355) Over time, internal divisions between various protest groups resulted in disintegration of the overall movement. However, Thai scholar Pavin Chachavalpongpun believed that the protests had already irrevocably expanded the space for criticism of the ruling elites, while Phil Robertson, deputy director of Human Rights Watch in Asia, thought that the resurgence of the activism could restart after the next general election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-356)

### Possibility of a coup

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Possibility of a coup") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section's source code: Possibility of a coup")\]

By September, protester pressure meant speculation about the possibility of a Thai military coup against Prayut increased, as his administration began to be viewed as increasingly untenable,[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-357) and rumors became rife when a tank was spotted in the streets; the military stated, as is usual on such occasions, that this was part of a previously scheduled military exercise.[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-358) However, this does not guarantee a more receptive audience to the protesters' demands as a palace-backed military faction is in ascendance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-359) BBC correspondent [Jonathan Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Head "Jonathan Head") wrote that the authorities could end their tolerance of the protests since the King had returned to Thailand and might actively pursue those who they believed to fund the protesters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc15Oct-1)[\[154\]](#refnote-bbc15Oct)

[Sonthi Limthongkul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonthi_Limthongkul "Sonthi Limthongkul"), former [People's Alliance for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Alliance_for_Democracy "People's Alliance for Democracy") leader, called for an intervention by the military, even though the prime minister and army commander dismissed its possibility.[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-360)

### Economic impact

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Economic impact") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section's source code: Economic impact")\]

Tamara Mast Henderson at Bloomberg argued that the unrest could lead to the change in the risk premium demanded by investors that hold Thai bonds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-361)

### "Judicial Coup" by the Constitutional Court

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=27 "Edit section: \"Judicial Coup\" by the Constitutional Court") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section's source code: \"Judicial Coup\" by the Constitutional Court")\]

On 10 August 2021, the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Thailand "Constitutional Court of Thailand") ruling that reforms to the monarchy is unconstitutional opened further discussion about politics landscape in Thailand. Sunai Phasuk of [Human Rights Watch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch "Human Rights Watch") described the ruling as "essentially a [judicial coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Coup "Constitutional Coup")" that could escalate more legal cases against protesters, possibility a treason. This could lead to an end to Thailand's [constitutional monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_monarchy "Constitutional monarchy") rule and replacing with [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy "Absolute monarchy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardiannovember-2)[\[42\]](#refnote-guardiannovember) [Jonathan Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Head "Jonathan Head") of [BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC") described this ruling forcefully shuts down any room in Thailand for public discussion of the monarchy, but private discussion and social media discussion will continue on, regardless of the government efforts to stop. It was seen as another politicised intervention on the side of conservatives, royalists, by the independent court.[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-362)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&veaction=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Explanatory notes") \| [edit source](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=2020%E2%80%932021_Thai_protests&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section's source code: Explanatory notes")\]

[&#8202;](#refnote-3)[↑](#ref-back-3-0)By 2022, street protests largely died down, which human rights groups have attributed to heavy suppression and surveillance by the Thai government, although activism has continued online due to being more difficult to prosecute.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto2-1)[\[1\]](#refnote-auto2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-1)[\[2\]](#refnote-auto1)
